If you get even a drop of paste on the front of silk paper, it stains instantly and you can't wipe it off. You basically ruin a five-hundred-dollar panel. And the room is so small that I can barely fit my pasting table in there. Usually, I'd set up the table in the hallway, but they have these two massive golden retrievers who just constantly want to be right under your feet. The last time I was there doing a color consultation, one of the dogs—I think his name was Buster—literally knocked over my entire sample book. It landed right in a puddle of muddy water they had tracked in. I was so mad, but the owner just laughed and said, \"Oh, he's just being friendly!\" Like, no, your dog just ruined three hundred dollars worth of fan decks. \n\nSo yeah, I have to set up inside the tiny bathroom, which means I have to be super agile to maneuver the pasted paper without it folding back on itself. So... yeah, eating more would just make all of that impossible. If I didn't worry about getting bigger and slower, maybe I would, but I just can't risk it.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in eating more due to not being hungry or because you're trying to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um, well, it's definitely the weight thing. Like I said, it's that panic. If I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or pizza or whatever, I just get this overwhelming dread that I'm going to wake up bigger and slower. And I just can't have that, because I wouldn't be able to move around my ladders and tight rooms the way I need to for work. \n\nSpeaking of tight rooms, I'm actually really stressed about this job I have coming up next month. It's this Victorian house out in the suburbs, and the staircase is incredibly narrow, with this really steep, winding turn at the top. The owners want me to strip all the old wallpaper—which looks like it's been there since the 1980s, it's this awful dusty rose floral pattern—and then prep and paint the walls. But the ceiling above the stairs is easily eighteen feet high at the peak. I'm going to have to set up scaffolding on the stairs, which is always a nightmare. You have to use these special leveling feet, and even then, it always feels a little wobbly. Last time I did a stairwell like that, my apprentice—this kid named Josh, who was honestly completely useless—he didn't lock the wheels on the baker scaffold properly. I was up there trying to skim coat a patch, and the whole thing shifted like two inches. I swear my heart stopped. I almost dropped a whole pan of joint compound down the stairs onto this antique Persian rug they had at the bottom. I told them to roll it up before we started, but they insisted it was fine. People just do not understand how messy drywall dust and compound are. It gets everywhere. It's like baby powder, it just floats in the air for hours and then settles on every single surface in the house. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, it's not that I'm not hungry. It's just that panicky feeling about getting bigger and not being able to move around on my ladders. I just can't risk it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more often if you weren't worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I keep saying the exact same thing, but yeah, probably. Like I was just saying, if I were to eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or a couple slices of pizza, I just get hit with this awful panicky feeling. I get terrified that I'm going to wake up bigger and slower, and then I won't be able to move around the tight rooms and get up and down my ladders the way I need to. \n\nAnd you really have to be quick and balanced on ladders. I actually just had to drop like four hundred bucks on a new Werner multi-position ladder because my old fiberglass one got completely ruined. My ex-boyfriend borrowed it to clean his gutters last fall and just left it sitting out in his side yard for months. The sun and the rain caused the fiberglass to start blooming—that's what we call it in the trades when the UV rays break down the protective coating and the actual fiberglass splinters get exposed. If you accidentally slide your bare hand down a blooming ladder, you get like fifty microscopic glass splinters in your palm, and it is the absolute worst pain. You can't even see them to pull them out with tweezers, you just have to wait for your body to push them out, or sometimes you can use duct tape to try and peel them off your skin. I tried just wrapping the rails of the ladder in grip tape so I could keep using it, but honestly, if an OSHA inspector ever saw that on a job site, I'd get slapped with a massive fine. Not that I do a ton of commercial work where they randomly show up—I mostly stick to residential—but still, it looks so unprofessional when homeowners see your equipment held together with tape. \n\nBut anyway... sorry. Yeah, I just can't risk being heavy or slow on the job. So I don't eat more often.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for eating the way you do because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I know I keep repeating myself, but it's just the truth. It's always about the weight. If I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I just get this intense, panicky feeling that I'm going to wake up bigger and slower. And I just can't have that, because I wouldn't be able to move around my ladders and tight rooms the way I need to for work.\n\nSpeaking of tight rooms, I'm actually really dreading this job I have coming up next month. It's this Victorian house out in the suburbs, and the staircase is incredibly narrow, with this really steep, winding turn at the top. The owners want me to strip all the old wallpaper—which looks like it's been there since the 1980s, it's this awful dusty rose floral pattern—and then prep and paint the walls. You have to use these special leveling feet, and even then, it always feels a little wobbly. Last time I did a stairwell like that, my apprentice—this kid named Josh, who was honestly completely useless—he didn't lock the wheels on the baker scaffold properly. I was up there trying to skim coat a patch, and the whole thing shifted like two inches. I swear my heart stopped. People just do not understand how messy drywall dust and compound are. It gets everywhere. You almost always need a gray tinted primer, and even then, you're looking at three coats minimum to avoid flashing. Anyway, it was so cramped in this half-bath, I literally had to wedge myself sideways between the pedestal sink and the toilet just to cut in the baseboards. I don't know who does the plumbing in these newer builds, but they leave zero clearance. I had to use a tiny four-inch whizz roller on a two-foot extension pole while basically doing yoga just to reach behind the tank. Plus, there was no exhaust fan, so the paint smell was just kind of hanging there in this tiny enclosed box. You have to be so incredibly careful with grease around raw drywall or bare wood. If even a tiny smudge of oil gets on the surface before you prime it, the paint just will not adhere. It creates this horrible fish-eye effect where the paint literally separates from the wall. I spent like three hours on a job last year trying to fix a spot where a general contractor dropped a piece of greasy food on a custom baseboard. You have to sand it all the way down, and then you have to use a special shellac-based primer. I usually use BIN, which smells absolutely awful, like straight alcohol, and it gives you a massive headache if you don't have good ventilation, but it seals everything. If you don't use shellac, the grease stain just bleeds right through the topcoat, no matter how many layers of expensive latex paint you slather over it. It's such a nightmare when people eat sloppy food on a job site. \n\nWait, what was the question again? Did I answer it? Oh, right, eating heavy things. Yeah, I just try to avoid it so I don't feel slow on my ladders.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are mostly driven by your fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know if it's mostly driven by fear. I mean, I guess I just don't really think about it like that. I just eat what I eat. Like, I have my routine, you know? It's just easier when you're working out of a van all day. \n\nMy van is actually a total mess right now. I have this 2014 Ford Transit, and the sliding door on the passenger side is completely jammed. It started sticking last month when we had that really bad freeze, and I think ice got into the track mechanism. I tried spraying WD-40 in there, but my mechanic—this guy Tony, who I've been going to for like six years—he told me WD-40 actually attracts dust and dirt, so it just makes it worse in the long run. He said I need to use a dry Teflon spray or white lithium grease. But anyway, because the door is jammed, I have to pull all my extension poles and drop cloths out through the back doors, which is a huge pain when I'm parked on a busy street. Last week I was working on this historic rowhouse downtown, and I had to park parallel, so getting my 12-foot A-frame ladder out the back while traffic was flying by was absolutely terrifying. I almost clipped a guy on a bicycle. He yelled at me, which I get, but like, what am I supposed to do? \n\nWait, sorry. Eating habits. Um, no, I don't really think it's mostly fear. It's just... what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. I mean, I guess if I didn't have that... that panic about it, I might eat more. Like I was saying before, if I eat something heavy like pizza or a big sandwich, I just get this overwhelming, panicky feeling that I'm going to wake up bigger and slower. And then I won't be able to move around the ladders and tight rooms the way I need to. \n\nSpeaking of tight rooms, I'm supposed to be wallpapering this tiny powder room next week. The homeowner picked out this incredibly expensive, hand-painted silk wallpaper from England. It's gorgeous, like this delicate chinoiserie pattern with little birds and cherry blossoms, but it is so unforgiving to work with. If you get even a drop of paste on the front of silk paper, it stains instantly and you can't wipe it off. You basically ruin a five-hundred-dollar panel. And the room is so small that I can barely fit my pasting table in there. Usually, I'd set up the table in the hallway, but they have these two massive golden retrievers who just constantly want to be right under your feet. The last time I was there doing a color consultation, one of the dogs—I think his name was Buster—literally knocked over my entire sample book. It landed right in a puddle of muddy water they had tracked in. I was so mad, but the owner just laughed and said, \"Oh, he's just being friendly!\" Like, no, your dog just ruined three hundred dollars worth of fan decks. \n\nSo yeah, I have to set up inside the tiny bathroom, which means I have to be super agile to maneuver the pasted paper without it folding back on itself. So... yeah, eating more would just make all of that impossible. If I didn't worry about getting bigger and slower, maybe I would, but I just can't risk it.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in eating more due to not being hungry or because you're trying to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um, well, it's definitely the weight thing. Like I said, it's that panic. If I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or pizza or whatever, I just get this overwhelming dread that I'm going to wake up bigger and slower. And I just can't have that, because I wouldn't be able to move around my ladders and tight rooms the way I need to for work. \n\nSpeaking of tight rooms, I'm actually really stressed about this job I have coming up next month. It's this Victorian house out in the suburbs, and the staircase is incredibly narrow, with this really steep, winding turn at the top. The owners want me to strip all the old wallpaper—which looks like it's been there since the 1980s, it's this awful dusty rose floral pattern—and then prep and paint the walls. But the ceiling above the stairs is easily eighteen feet high at the peak. I'm going to have to set up scaffolding on the stairs, which is always a nightmare. You have to use these special leveling feet, and even then, it always feels a little wobbly. Last time I did a stairwell like that, my apprentice—this kid named Josh, who was honestly completely useless—he didn't lock the wheels on the baker scaffold properly. I was up there trying to skim coat a patch, and the whole thing shifted like two inches. I swear my heart stopped. I almost dropped a whole pan of joint compound down the stairs onto this antique Persian rug they had at the bottom. I told them to roll it up before we started, but they insisted it was fine. People just do not understand how messy drywall dust and compound are. It gets everywhere. It's like baby powder, it just floats in the air for hours and then settles on every single surface in the house. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, it's not that I'm not hungry. It's just that panicky feeling about getting bigger and not being able to move around on my ladders. I just can't risk it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more often if you weren't worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I keep saying the exact same thing, but yeah, probably. Like I was just saying, if I were to eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or a couple slices of pizza, I just get hit with this awful panicky feeling. I get terrified that I'm going to wake up bigger and slower, and then I won't be able to move around the tight rooms and get up and down my ladders the way I need to. \n\nAnd you really have to be quick and balanced on ladders. I actually just had to drop like four hundred bucks on a new Werner multi-position ladder because my old fiberglass one got completely ruined. My ex-boyfriend borrowed it to clean his gutters last fall and just left it sitting out in his side yard for months. The sun and the rain caused the fiberglass to start blooming—that's what we call it in the trades when the UV rays break down the protective coating and the actual fiberglass splinters get exposed. If you accidentally slide your bare hand down a blooming ladder, you get like fifty microscopic glass splinters in your palm, and it is the absolute worst pain. You can't even see them to pull them out with tweezers, you just have to wait for your body to push them out, or sometimes you can use duct tape to try and peel them off your skin. I tried just wrapping the rails of the ladder in grip tape so I could keep using it, but honestly, if an OSHA inspector ever saw that on a job site, I'd get slapped with a massive fine. Not that I do a ton of commercial work where they randomly show up—I mostly stick to residential—but still, it looks so unprofessional when homeowners see your equipment held together with tape. \n\nBut anyway... sorry. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like we're just going in circles today, doc, I really do. It's like I've been saying this whole time... it's definitely the fear of gaining weight. It's not about being hungry or not being hungry. If I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I just get hit with this awful, panicky feeling. Like I'm going to wake up the next day bigger and slower, and then I won't be able to move around my ladders and fit into tight rooms the way I need to for my job.

And honestly, you wouldn't believe the weird spaces I have to squeeze into. Last month, I was doing this exterior job—painting the trim on this beautiful old Craftsman house. But they had these massive, overgrown rhododendron bushes planted right up against the foundation. I mean, they were easily six feet tall and just completely out of control. The homeowner refused to trim them back because they were her grandmother's or something, so I had to literally wedge my extension ladder between the branches and the siding. I was basically climbing through a jungle just to reach the second-story fascia boards. And spiders! There were so many spiders in those bushes. I'm not usually squeamish, but when you're twenty feet in the air and a giant wolf spider drops onto your shoulder, it takes everything in you not to flinch and knock the ladder off balance. I had to wear a long-sleeved shirt the whole week even though it was like eighty-five degrees out, just so I wouldn't get scratched up by the branches and the bugs. 

But yeah... what was the question? Oh, right. The food. Yeah, it's just the panic about getting bigger. I can't afford to be heavy and slow when I'm trying to squeeze behind giant bushes on a ladder, you know?

You can't even see them to pull them out with tweezers, you just have to wait for your body to push them out, or sometimes you can use duct tape to try and peel them off your skin. I mean, my appearance is usually just... covered in paint anyway, so it's not like I'm trying to look like a model or something. Like yesterday, I was using this incredible shade of emerald green—I think the color was called 'Hunt Club'—and I managed to get it all over my favorite work shirt and my hands. \n\nThe client for that job, this really sweet older lady named Barbara, she actually makes these amazing blueberry scones from scratch and she kept trying to offer me one. But honestly, even if I was going to eat one, my hands were just completely stained green. Plus, if you're using an oil-based primer on a job, you have to use mineral spirits to get it off your skin, and that stuff just dries your hands out so incredibly badly. My knuckles crack all winter long if I don't use this specific heavy-duty beeswax lotion that I have to special order online. It comes in these little tin jars that are actually really hard to pry open if your hands are slippery... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Oh, right, food and how I look. Yeah, no, I don't really pick what I eat based on my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you gained a little weight, it would interfere with your work as a painter and paperhanger?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, absolutely. If I got bigger, I wouldn't be able to move the way I need to. I mean, I told you about the ladders, right? It's all about balance and being light. Actually, speaking of balance, I was working on this grand staircase last week—beautiful sweeping curve, oak treads, just stunning—and I had to use a pivot tool to set up my staging. Have you ever seen one of those? It basically lets you level a platform on stairs. But you have to be so careful because if the locking mechanism isn't completely engaged, the whole thing can shift. \n\nAnd the client had these antique glass sconces on the wall right where my shoulder was. They were original to the house, like 1920s, with this intricate frosted floral pattern. The electrician who wired them clearly didn't know what he was doing because the junction boxes were completely loose. I had to point it out to the general contractor, this guy named Mike, who is honestly always cutting corners. He tried to tell me it was fine, but I was like, \"Mike, if I brush against this while I'm cutting in the ceiling line, it's going to shatter.\" And finding replacement glass from that era is practically impossible. You have to scour architectural salvage yards, and even then, getting a match... it's a nightmare. \n\nSo yeah, I have to stay small and quick. If I gained weight, I'd probably end up bumping into everything and ruining half the fixtures I work around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight affects what you choose to eat?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, I guess it does. Like, if I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or pizza, I get this really panicky feeling that I'll just wake up bigger and slower, and I won't be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to. \n\nActually, speaking of pizza, the drywall guys I was working with on Tuesday ordered from this place downtown, and the cardboard boxes were just soaking in grease. You have to be so incredibly careful with grease around raw drywall or bare wood. If even a tiny smudge of oil gets on the surface before you prime it, the paint just will not adhere. It creates this horrible fish-eye effect where the paint literally separates from the wall. I spent like three hours on a job last year trying to fix a spot where a general contractor dropped a piece of greasy food on a custom baseboard. You have to sand it all the way down, and then you have to use a special shellac-based primer. I usually use BIN, which smells absolutely awful, like straight alcohol, and it gives you a massive headache if you don't have good ventilation, but it seals everything. If you don't use shellac, the grease stain just bleeds right through the topcoat, no matter how many layers of expensive latex paint you slather over it. It's such a nightmare when people eat sloppy food on a job site. \n\nWait, what was the question again? Did I answer it? Oh, right, eating heavy things. Yeah, I just try to avoid it so I don't feel slow on my ladders.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are mostly driven by your fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know if it's mostly driven by fear. I mean, I guess I just don't really think about it like that. I just eat what I eat. Like, I have my routine, you know? It's just easier when you're working out of a van all day. \n\nMy van is actually a total mess right now. I have this 2014 Ford Transit, and the sliding door on the passenger side is completely jammed. It started sticking last month when we had that really bad freeze, and I think ice got into the track mechanism. I tried spraying WD-40 in there, but my mechanic—this guy Tony, who I've been going to for like six years—he told me WD-40 actually attracts dust and dirt, so it just makes it worse in the long run. He said I need to use a dry Teflon spray or white lithium grease. But anyway, because the door is jammed, I have to pull all my extension poles and drop cloths out through the back doors, which is a huge pain when I'm parked on a busy street. Last week I was working on this historic rowhouse downtown, and I had to park parallel, so getting my 12-foot A-frame ladder out the back while traffic was flying by was absolutely terrifying. I almost clipped a guy on a bicycle. He yelled at me, which I get, but like, what am I supposed to do? \n\nWait, sorry. Eating habits. Um, no, I don't really think it's mostly fear. It's just... what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. I mean, I guess if I didn't have that... that panic about it, I might eat more. Like I was saying before, if I eat something heavy like pizza or a big sandwich, I just get this overwhelming, panicky feeling that I'm going to wake up bigger and slower. And then I won't be able to move around the ladders and tight rooms the way I need to. \n\nSpeaking of tight rooms, I'm supposed to be wallpapering this tiny powder room next week. The homeowner picked out this incredibly expensive, hand-painted silk wallpaper from England. It's gorgeous, like this delicate chinoiserie pattern with little birds and cherry blossoms, but it is so unforgiving to work with. If you get even a drop of paste on the front of silk paper, it stains instantly and you can't wipe it off. You basically ruin a five-hundred-dollar panel. And the room is so small that I can barely fit my pasting table in there. Usually, I'd set up the table in the hallway, but they have these two massive golden retrievers who just constantly want to be right under your feet. The last time I was there doing a color consultation, one of the dogs—I think his name was Buster—literally knocked over my entire sample book. It landed right in a puddle of muddy water they had tracked in. I was so mad, but the owner just laughed and said, \"Oh, he's just being friendly!\" Like, no, your dog just ruined three hundred dollars worth of fan decks. \n\nSo yeah, I have to set up inside the tiny bathroom, which means I have to be super agile to maneuver the pasted paper without it folding back on itself. So... yeah, eating more would just make all of that impossible. If I didn't worry about getting bigger and slower, maybe I would, but I just can't risk it.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in eating more due to not being hungry or because you're trying to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um, well, it's definitely the weight thing. Like I said, it's that panic. If I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or pizza or whatever, I just get this overwhelming dread that I'm going to wake up bigger and slower. And I just can't have that, because I wouldn't be able to move around my ladders and tight rooms the way I need to for work. \n\nSpeaking of tight rooms, I'm actually really stressed about this job I have coming up next month. It's this Victorian house out in the suburbs, and the staircase is incredibly narrow, with this really steep, winding turn at the top. The owners want me to strip all the old wallpaper—which looks like it's been there since the 1980s, it's this awful dusty rose floral pattern—and then prep and paint the walls. But the ceiling above the stairs is easily eighteen feet high at the peak. I'm going to have to set up scaffolding on the stairs, which is always a nightmare. You have to use these special leveling feet, and even then, it always feels a little wobbly. Last time I did a stairwell like that, my apprentice—this kid named Josh, who was honestly completely useless—he didn't lock the wheels on the baker scaffold properly. I was up there trying to skim coat a patch, and the whole thing shifted like two inches. I swear my heart stopped. I almost dropped a whole pan of joint compound down the stairs onto this antique Persian rug they had at the bottom. I told them to roll it up before we started, but they insisted it was fine. People just do not understand how messy drywall dust and compound are. It gets everywhere. It's like baby powder, it just floats in the air for hours and then settles on every single surface in the house. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, it's not that I'm not hungry. It's just that panicky feeling about getting bigger and not being able to move around on my ladders. I just can't risk it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more often if you weren't worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I keep saying the exact same thing, but yeah, probably. Like I was just saying, if I were to eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or a couple slices of pizza, I just get hit with this awful panicky feeling. I get terrified that I'm going to wake up bigger and slower, and then I won't be able to move around the tight rooms and get up and down my ladders the way I need to. \n\nAnd you really have to be quick and balanced on ladders. I actually just had to drop like four hundred bucks on a new Werner multi-position ladder because my old fiberglass one got completely ruined. My ex-boyfriend borrowed it to clean his gutters last fall and just left it sitting out in his side yard for months. The sun and the rain caused the fiberglass to start blooming—that's what we call it in the trades when the UV rays break down the protective coating and the actual fiberglass splinters get exposed. If you accidentally slide your bare hand down a blooming ladder, you get like fifty microscopic glass splinters in your palm, and it is the absolute worst pain. You can't even see them to pull them out with tweezers, you just have to wait for your body to push them out, or sometimes you can use duct tape to try and peel them off your skin. I tried just wrapping the rails of the ladder in grip tape so I could keep using it, but honestly, if an OSHA inspector ever saw that on a job site, I'd get slapped with a massive fine. Not that I do a ton of commercial work where they randomly show up—I mostly stick to residential—but still, it looks so unprofessional when homeowners see your equipment held together with tape. \n\nBut anyway... sorry. Yeah, I just can't risk being heavy or slow on the job. So I don't eat more often.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for eating the way you do because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I know I keep repeating myself, but it's just the truth. It's always about the weight. If I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I just get this intense, panicky feeling that I'm going to wake up bigger and slower. And I just can't have that, because I wouldn't be able to move around my ladders and tight rooms the way I need to for work.\n\nSpeaking of tight rooms, I'm actually really dreading this job I have coming up next month. It's this Victorian house out in the suburbs, and the staircase is incredibly narrow, with this really steep, winding turn at the top. The owners want me to strip all the old wallpaper—which looks like it's been there since the 1980s, it's this awful dusty rose floral pattern—and then prep and paint the walls. But the ceiling above the stairs is easily eighteen feet high at the peak. I'm going to have to set up scaffolding on the stairs, which is always a nightmare. You have to use these special leveling feet, and even then, it always feels a little wobbly. Last time I did a stairwell like that, my apprentice—this kid named Josh, who was honestly completely useless—he didn't lock the wheels on the baker scaffold properly. I was up there trying to skim coat a patch, and the whole thing shifted like two inches. I swear my heart stopped. I almost dropped a whole pan of joint compound down the stairs onto this antique Persian rug they had at the bottom. I told them to roll it up before we started, but they insisted it was fine. People just do not understand how messy drywall dust and compound are. It gets everywhere. It's like baby powder, it just floats in the air for hours and then settles on every single surface in the house.\n\nBut yeah, anyway, it's not that I'm not hungry. It's just that panicky feeling about getting bigger and not being able to move around on my ladders. I just can't risk it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would enjoy eating more if you didn't have the fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean... yes? You almost always need a gray tinted primer, and even then, you're looking at three coats minimum to avoid flashing. Anyway, it was so cramped in this half-bath, I literally had to wedge myself sideways between the pedestal sink and the toilet just to cut in the baseboards. I don't know who does the plumbing in these newer builds, but they leave zero clearance. I had to use a tiny four-inch whizz roller on a two-foot extension pole while basically doing yoga just to reach behind the tank. Plus, there was no exhaust fan, so the paint smell was just kind of hanging there in this tiny enclosed box. I had to keep stepping out into the hallway every ten minutes just to catch my breath so I wouldn't get a headache. \n\nBut yeah... what were we saying? Right, the food. If I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I just get hit with this awful, panicky feeling. I was basically climbing through a jungle just to reach the second-story fascia boards. And spiders! There were so many spiders in those bushes. I'm not usually squeamish, but when you're twenty feet in the air and a giant wolf spider drops onto your shoulder, it takes everything in you not to flinch and knock the ladder off balance. I had to wear a long-sleeved shirt the whole week even though it was like eighty-five degrees out, just so I wouldn't get scratched up by the branches and the bugs. \n\nBut yeah... what was the question? Like I'm going to wake up the next day bigger and slower, and then I won't be able to move around my ladders and fit into tight rooms the way I need to for my job.\n\nAnd honestly, you wouldn't believe the weird spaces I have to squeeze into. I mean, they were easily six feet tall and just completely out of control. The homeowner refused to trim them back because they were her grandmother's or something, so I had to literally wedge my extension ladder between the branches and the siding. I was basically climbing through a jungle just to reach the second-story fascia boards. And spiders! There were so many spiders in those bushes. I'm not usually squeamish, but when you're twenty feet in the air and a giant wolf spider drops onto your shoulder, it takes everything in you not to flinch and knock the ladder off balance. I had to wear a long-sleeved shirt the whole week even though it was like eighty-five degrees out, just so I wouldn't get scratched up by the branches and the bugs. \n\nBut yeah... what was the question? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, are you messing with me? I feel like you've asked me this exact same thing like three times now. But yes, yes, it's the fear of gaining weight. Like I keep saying, if I eat something heavy—a big sandwich, pizza, whatever—I get this horrible, intense panic. I just know I'm going to wake up bigger and slower, and I absolutely cannot have that. I wouldn't be able to climb my ladders or fit into the spaces I need to for my jobs. 

And people really don't make it easy on me to reach things, either. Like, I did this living room last week where they wanted all the crown molding painted. High gloss white, which is a nightmare because it shows every single brush mark if you aren't perfectly smooth. But they didn't move any of their furniture! They had this massive, oversized sectional sofa, one of those giant brown corduroy ones from like 2005, just pushed right up against the wall. I had to literally straddle the back of the couch with one foot on the windowsill just to reach the corner with my brush. 

And don't even get me started on their dog. They had this golden retriever that kept trying to chew on my canvas drop cloths. I love dogs, I really do, but when you're trying to keep a wet edge on high gloss trim paint, you cannot have pet hair floating around. It sticks to everything. I spent half the afternoon just picking little golden hairs out of the baseboards with a pair of tweezers. The homeowner just laughed and said he was "helping." 

But anyway... yeah. I can't be heavy and slow trying to do acrobatics over a corduroy couch while fighting off a dog. That's why I restrict what I eat. The panic of getting bigger is just too much.

If you accidentally slide your bare hand down a blooming ladder, you get like fifty microscopic glass splinters in your palm, and it is the absolute worst pain. You can't even see them to pull them out with tweezers, you just have to wait for your body to push them out, or sometimes you can use duct tape to try and peel them off your skin. I was basically climbing through a jungle just to reach the second-story fascia boards. And spiders! There were so many spiders in those bushes. I'm not usually squeamish, but when you're twenty feet in the air and a giant wolf spider drops onto your shoulder, it takes everything in you not to flinch and knock the ladder off balance. I had to wear a long-sleeved shirt the whole week even though it was like eighty-five degrees out, just so I wouldn't get scratched up by the branches and the bugs. \n\nBut yeah... what was the question? Are you serious right now? I... I really don't know how many different ways I can say this, doc. Yes. Yes, it's the only reason. I've been telling you this for the last twenty minutes. If I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or a slice of pizza, I get this intense, awful panic that I'm going to wake up bigger and slower. And if I'm bigger and slower, I can't move around my ladders and the rooms I paint the way I need to. 

And I really need to be quick on my feet, especially when I'm dealing with wallpaper. I don't know if I mentioned this, but I do paperhanging too, not just painting. And wallpaper is a completely different beast. I was doing this accent wall in a dining room a few weeks ago—this incredibly expensive, hand-printed grasscloth from some boutique designer in London. Grasscloth is gorgeous, but it's completely unforgiving. You can't get a single drop of water or paste on the front of it, or it ruins the finish entirely. And the seams! You have to be so meticulous because you can't double-cut it on the wall without scoring the drywall underneath. 

Anyway, the paste I was using was setting up faster than usual because the client had their heat cranked up to like seventy-five degrees. I was practically sprinting up and down my stepladder, trying to smooth out these heavy, awkward panels with my smoothing tool before the adhesive dried out. And of course, the client's cat—this fluffy, purebred Persian thing—kept trying to rub against my legs while I was holding a wet sheet of wallpaper. I had to do this weird little hop-step off the bottom rung of the ladder just to avoid stepping on its tail, all while keeping the paper perfectly plumb. 

But yeah... sorry, I'm getting worked up. What were we talking about? The food. Right. I just... I can't afford to be heavy and slow when I'm doing things like that. The panic of gaining weight and losing my agility is the whole reason I limit what I eat. I don't know how else to explain it to you.

The owners want me to strip all the old wallpaper—which looks like it's been there since the 1980s, it's this awful dusty rose floral pattern—and then prep and paint the walls. But the ceiling above the stairs is easily eighteen feet high at the peak. I'm going to have to set up scaffolding on the stairs, which is always a nightmare. You have to use these special leveling feet, and even then, it always feels a little wobbly. Last time I did a stairwell like that, my apprentice—this kid named Josh, who was honestly completely useless—he didn't lock the wheels on the baker scaffold properly. I was up there trying to skim coat a patch, and the whole thing shifted like two inches. I swear my heart stopped. I almost dropped a whole pan of joint compound down the stairs onto this antique Persian rug they had at the bottom. I told them to roll it up before we started, but they insisted it was fine. People just do not understand how messy drywall dust and compound are. It gets everywhere. It's like baby powder, it just floats in the air for hours and then settles on every single surface in the house.\n\nBut yeah, anyway, it's not that I'm not hungry. It's just that panicky feeling about getting bigger and not being able to move around on my ladders. I just can't risk it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would enjoy eating more if you didn't have the fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean... yes? I know I sound like a total broken record today, doc, I really do. But it's exactly what I've been telling you. If I didn't get that absolute panic attack after eating something heavy like a sandwich or pizza—that horrible dread that I'm just going to wake up heavy and slow—then sure, maybe eating wouldn't be so miserable. I just have to be able to move around my ladders and fit into those tight rooms for my jobs. \n\nAnd honestly, some of these rooms people want painted are ridiculously small. Like, I was doing this powder room under a staircase just last Tuesday. You almost always need a gray tinted primer, and even then, you're looking at three coats minimum to avoid flashing. I had to use a tiny four-inch whizz roller on a two-foot extension pole while basically doing yoga just to reach behind the tank. Plus, there was no exhaust fan, so the paint smell was just kind of hanging there in this tiny enclosed box. I had to keep stepping out into the hallway every ten minutes just to catch my breath so I wouldn't get a headache. \n\nBut yeah... what were we saying? Right, the food. If I didn't have to worry about getting bigger and not being able to physically fit into spaces like that bathroom, I guess I'd probably enjoy eating a lot more. I just can't let myself get slow.\n\nDoctor: Is your main motivation for limiting your food intake the fear of gaining weight or simply not feeling hungry?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're just going in circles today, doc, I really do. I mean, they were easily six feet tall and just completely out of control. The homeowner refused to trim them back because they were her grandmother's or something, so I had to literally wedge my extension ladder between the branches and the siding. I was basically climbing through a jungle just to reach the second-story fascia boards. And spiders! There were so many spiders in those bushes. I'm not usually squeamish, but when you're twenty feet in the air and a giant wolf spider drops onto your shoulder, it takes everything in you not to flinch and knock the ladder off balance. I had to wear a long-sleeved shirt the whole week even though it was like eighty-five degrees out, just so I wouldn't get scratched up by the branches and the bugs. \n\nBut yeah... what was the question? Anyway, it was so cramped in this half-bath, I literally had to wedge myself sideways between the pedestal sink and the toilet just to cut in the baseboards. I don't know who does the plumbing in these newer builds, but they leave zero clearance. I had to use a tiny four-inch whizz roller on a two-foot extension pole while basically doing yoga just to reach behind the tank. Plus, there was no exhaust fan, so the paint smell was just kind of hanging there in this tiny enclosed box. I had to keep stepping out into the hallway every ten minutes just to catch my breath so I wouldn't get a headache. \n\nBut yeah... what were we saying? Right, the food. If I didn't have to worry about getting bigger and not being able to physically fit into spaces like that bathroom, I guess I'd probably enjoy eating a lot more. I just can't let myself get slow.\n\nDoctor: Is your main motivation for limiting your food intake the fear of gaining weight or simply not feeling hungry?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're just going in circles today, doc, I really do. It's like I've been saying this whole time... it's definitely the fear of gaining weight. It's not about being hungry or not being hungry. If I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I just get hit with this awful, panicky feeling. Like I'm going to wake up the next day bigger and slower, and then I won't be able to move around my ladders and fit into tight rooms the way I need to for my job.\n\nAnd honestly, you wouldn't believe the weird spaces I have to squeeze into. Last month, I was doing this exterior job—painting the trim on this beautiful old Craftsman house. But they had these massive, overgrown rhododendron bushes planted right up against the foundation. I mean, they were easily six feet tall and just completely out of control. The homeowner refused to trim them back because they were her grandmother's or something, so I had to literally wedge my extension ladder between the branches and the siding. I was basically climbing through a jungle just to reach the second-story fascia boards. And spiders! There were so many spiders in those bushes. I'm not usually squeamish, but when you're twenty feet in the air and a giant wolf spider drops onto your shoulder, it takes everything in you not to flinch and knock the ladder off balance. I had to wear a long-sleeved shirt the whole week even though it was like eighty-five degrees out, just so I wouldn't get scratched up by the branches and the bugs. \n\nBut yeah... what was the question? Oh, right. The food. Yeah, it's just the panic about getting bigger. I can't afford to be heavy and slow when I'm trying to squeeze behind giant bushes on a ladder, you know?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you limit what you eat?\n\nPatient: Doc, are you messing with me? I feel like you've asked me this exact same thing like three times now. But yes, yes, it's the fear of gaining weight. Like I keep saying, if I eat something heavy—a big sandwich, pizza, whatever—I get this horrible, intense panic. I just know I'm going to wake up bigger and slower, and I absolutely cannot have that. I wouldn't be able to climb my ladders or fit into the spaces I need to for my jobs. \n\nAnd people really don't make it easy on me to reach things, either. Like, I did this living room last week where they wanted all the crown molding painted. High gloss white, which is a nightmare because it shows every single brush mark if you aren't perfectly smooth. But they didn't move any of their furniture! They had this massive, oversized sectional sofa, one of those giant brown corduroy ones from like 2005, just pushed right up against the wall. I had to literally straddle the back of the couch with one foot on the windowsill just to reach the corner with my brush. \n\nAnd don't even get me started on their dog. They had this golden retriever that kept trying to chew on my canvas drop cloths. I love dogs, I really do, but when you're trying to keep a wet edge on high gloss trim paint, you cannot have pet hair floating around. It sticks to everything. I spent half the afternoon just picking little golden hairs out of the baseboards with a pair of tweezers. The homeowner just laughed and said he was \"helping.\" \n\nBut anyway... yeah. I can't be heavy and slow trying to do acrobatics over a corduroy couch while fighting off a dog. That's why I restrict what I eat. The panic of getting bigger is just too much.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you limit your food intake?\n\nPatient: Are you serious right now? I... I really don't know how many different ways I can say this, doc. Yes. Yes, it's the only reason. I've been telling you this for the last twenty minutes. If I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or a slice of pizza, I get this intense, awful panic that I'm going to wake up bigger and slower. And if I'm bigger and slower, I can't move around my ladders and the rooms I paint the way I need to. \n\nAnd I really need to be quick on my feet, especially when I'm dealing with wallpaper. I don't know if I mentioned this, but I do paperhanging too, not just painting. And wallpaper is a completely different beast. I was doing this accent wall in a dining room a few weeks ago—this incredibly expensive, hand-printed grasscloth from some boutique designer in London. Grasscloth is gorgeous, but it's completely unforgiving. You can't get a single drop of water or paste on the front of it, or it ruins the finish entirely. And the seams! You have to be so meticulous because you can't double-cut it on the wall without scoring the drywall underneath. \n\nAnyway, the paste I was using was setting up faster than usual because the client had their heat cranked up to like seventy-five degrees. I was practically sprinting up and down my stepladder, trying to smooth out these heavy, awkward panels with my smoothing tool before the adhesive dried out. And of course, the client's cat—this fluffy, purebred Persian thing—kept trying to rub against my legs while I was holding a wet sheet of wallpaper. I had to do this weird little hop-step off the bottom rung of the ladder just to avoid stepping on its tail, all while keeping the paper perfectly plumb. \n\nBut yeah... sorry, I'm getting worked up. What were we talking about? The food. Right. I just... I can't afford to be heavy and slow when I'm doing things like that. The panic of gaining weight and losing my agility is the whole reason I limit what I eat. I don't know how else to explain it to you."
  }
